Notation and definitions

We follow the setup of Section 3.11 in
van der Vaart and Wellner (1996)
. Throughout, we index by
K
, with the total sample size
N:=N(K):=\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}
growing implicitly with
K
. Recall that we observe
N
data units
O^{(K)}=\{O_{ki}:1\leq i\leq n_{k},\ 1\leq k\leq K\}
, drawn jointly from a distribution
P^{(K)}
, where each unit
O_{ki}=(Z_{ki}=k,X_{ki},Y_{ki})\in\mathcal{O}
. The observations
O_{ki}
are mutually independent but not identically distributed. Let
(\mathcal{O}^{K},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}^{K}))
denote the Borel measurable space on which
P^{(K)}
is defined, corresponding to the product space
\mathcal{O}^{K}:=\bigotimes_{k=1}^{K}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\mathcal{O}
. Our statistical model
\mathcal{P}^{(K)}
consists of all distributions on
\mathcal{O}^{K}
, dominated by a common product measure
\nu^{(K)}
induced by measures
\nu_{k}^{(K)}
defined on the support of
O_{ki}
, that satisfy the independence assumptions described in Section
2
.

Let
P_{k}^{(K)}
denote the marginal distribution of
O_{ki}=(Z_{ki}=k,X_{ki},Y_{ki})
under
P^{(K)}
, which does not depend on
i
. Let
p_{k}^{(K)}:=\frac{dP_{k}^{(K)}}{d\nu_{k}^{(K)}}
and
p^{(K)}:=\frac{dP^{(K)}}{d\nu^{(K)}}
denote the corresponding densities. Then, for each realization
o^{(K)}
of
O^{(K)}
, we can write, by mutual independence,
p^{(K)}(o^{(K)})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\prod_{i=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k}^{(K)}(o_{ki}),

and the log-likelihood satisfies

\displaystyle\log p^{(K)}(o^{(K)})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\log p_{k}^{(K)}(o_{ki}).

(15)

We now recall the definition of a regular parametric submodel. Let
\{P_{t}^{(K)}:|t|\leq\delta\}\subset\mathcal{P}^{(K)}
be a one-dimensional parametric submodel through
P^{(K)}\in\mathcal{P}^{(K)}
, with corresponding densities
p_{t}^{(K)}=dP_{t}^{(K)}/d\nu
. The submodel is said to be
quadratic mean differentiable
(QMD) at
t=0
if there exists a measurable function
s_{P^{(K)}}\in L^{2}(P^{(K)})
, called the score function, such that

\int_{\mathcal{O}^{K}}\left(\sqrt{p_{t}^{(K)}(o^{(K)})}-\sqrt{p^{(K)}(o^{(K)})}-\tfrac{t}{2}s_{P^{(K)}}(o^{(K)})\sqrt{p^{(K)}(o^{(K)})}\right)^{2}d\nu^{(K)}(o^{(K)})=o(t^{2})\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }t\to 0.

We refer to
s_{P^{(K)}}
as the score of the submodel at
t=0
, and define the Fisher information as
I_{P^{(K)}}:=\int s_{P^{(K)}}^{2}(o^{(K)})\,dP^{(K)}(o^{(K)})
. We review standard results on quadratic mean differentiability for factorizable models
(
Bickel et al., 1993
)
. The submodel density admits the factorization
p_{t}^{(K)}(o^{(K)})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\prod_{i=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k,t}^{(K)}(o_{ki}),

and the score function admits a unique decomposition
s_{P^{(K)}}(o^{(K)})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o_{ki}),

where each
s_{k,P^{(K)}}\in L^{2}_{0}(P_{k}^{(K)})
, since independence induces an orthogonal decomposition of the model tangent space. Moreover, each factor submodel
t\mapsto p_{k,t}^{(K)}
is QMD with score function
s_{k,P^{(K)}}
, satisfying

\int_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sqrt{p_{k,t}^{(K)}(o)}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}-\tfrac{t}{2}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}\right)^{2}d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)=o(t^{2})\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }t\to 0.

In fact, the converse also holds: the full density
p_{t}^{(K)}
is QMD at
t=0
if and only if each of the factor densities
p_{k,t}^{(K)}
is QMD at
t=0
.

We showed in Theorem
9
that the parameter
\Psi^{(K)}:\mathcal{P}^{(K)}\to\mathbb{R}
is
pathwise differentiable
at
P^{(K)}\in\mathcal{P}^{(K)}
, with efficient influence function (EIF)
D_{P^{(K)}}^{*}\in L_{0}^{2}(P^{(K)})
. In this setting, the EIF
D_{P^{(K)}}^{*}
is defined as a function of the full observed data vector
O^{(K)}
, rather than of a single observation. However, due to independence across data units, the EIF admits a decomposition of the form

D_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O^{(K)})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi^{*}_{P^{(K)}}(O_{ki}),

where each summand
\varphi^{*}_{P^{(K)}}\in L_{0}^{2}(P_{k}^{(K)})
has mean zero and finite variance under the distribution
P_{k}^{(K)}
of
O_{ki}
. This decomposition follows directly from Theorem
9
.

I.2
Existence of Fisher-normalizable sequence of least favorable submodels

We begin by establishing the existence of a least favorable sequence of submodels that satisfies Definition
1
. To this end, we use the following construction of a QMD submodel, which we refer to as the Le Cam–Hájek QMD path, as it is directly motivated by the definition of quadratic mean differentiability.

Lemma 17

(Le Cam–Hájek QMD path for the
k
th density factor)
.

Let
p_{k}^{(K)}
be the marginal density of
P^{(K)}
with respect to
\nu_{k}^{(K)}
, and let
s_{k,P^{(K)}}\in L_{0}^{2}(P_{k}^{(K)})
with Fisher information
I_{k,P^{(K)}}:=\int s_{k,P^{(K)}}^{2}(o)\,dP_{k}^{(K)}(o)<\infty
. Define the path

p_{k,t}^{(K)}:=\frac{\left(\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}+\tfrac{t}{2}\,s_{k,P^{(K)}}\,\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}\right)^{2}}{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}.

Then
\{p_{k,t}^{(K)}:|t|\leq\delta\}
is QMD at
t=0
with score
s_{k,P^{(K)}}
and satisfies

\left\|\sqrt{p_{k,t}^{(K)}}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}-\tfrac{t}{2}\,s_{k,P^{(K)}}\,\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{k}^{(K)})}^{2}=\left(1-\sqrt{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}\right)^{2}\leq I_{k,P^{(K)}}^{2}t^{4}.

Proof.

Let
q(o):=\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}
and write
q_{t}(o)\;:=\;q(o)+\frac{t}{2}\,s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o).

Since

\int q(o)^{2}\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)=1,\hskip 18.49988pt\int q(o)\bigl[s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)\bigr]\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)=\int s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,p_{k}^{(K)}(o)\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)=0,

and
\int\bigl[s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)\bigr]^{2}\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)=\int s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)^{2}\,p_{k}^{(K)}(o)\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)=I_{k,P^{(K)}},

we have

Z_{k,t}\;:=\;\int q_{t}(o)^{2}\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)\;=\;\int\Bigl[q(o)+\tfrac{t}{2}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)\Bigr]^{2}\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}\;=\;1+\frac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}.

Hence
q_{t}^{2}/Z_{k,t}
is a valid density on
\mathcal{O}
for
|t|<\infty
. Define

p_{k,t}^{(K)}(o)\;=\;\frac{q_{t}(o)^{2}}{Z_{k,t}}=\frac{\bigl[q(o)+\tfrac{t}{2}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)\bigr]^{2}}{\,1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}\,}\,.

Then
p_{k,0}^{(K)}=p_{k}^{(K)}
. Moreover,

\log p_{k,t}^{(K)}(o)=2\log\bigl[q(o)+\tfrac{t}{2}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)\bigr]-\log\Bigl(1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}\Bigr).

Differentiating at
t=0
gives

\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right|_{t=0}\log p_{k,t}^{(K)}(o)=2\,\frac{\tfrac{1}{2}\,s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)}{q(o)}-0=s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o).

Thus the score at
t=0
is
s_{k,P^{(K)}}
. Next,

\sqrt{p_{k,t}^{(K)}(o)}=\frac{q_{t}(o)}{\sqrt{Z_{k,t}}}=\frac{q(o)+\tfrac{t}{2}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)}{\sqrt{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}}.

Define the pointwise remainder

R_{k,t}(o):=\sqrt{p_{k,t}^{(K)}(o)}-q(o)-\frac{t}{2}\,s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o).

Then

\displaystyle R_{k,t}(o)

\displaystyle=\frac{q(o)+\tfrac{t}{2}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)}{\sqrt{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}}-q(o)-\frac{t}{2}\,s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(q(o)+\tfrac{t}{2}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)\Bigr)\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}}-1\Bigr).

Hence

\bigl\lVert R_{k,t}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\nu_{k}^{(K)})}^{2}=\int R_{k,t}(o)^{2}\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)=\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}}-1\Bigr)^{2}\int\Bigl[q(o)+\tfrac{t}{2}s_{k,P^{(K)}}(o)\,q(o)\Bigr]^{2}\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o).

Since
\int[q_{t}(o)]^{2}\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}=Z_{k,t}=1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}
, it follows that

\bigl\lVert R_{k,t}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\nu_{k}^{(K)})}^{2}=\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}}-1\Bigr)^{2}\Bigl(1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}\Bigr)=\Bigl(1-\sqrt{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}\Bigr)^{2}.

Since
|1-\sqrt{1+x}|\leq x
, we have

\bigl\lVert R_{k,t}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}(\nu_{k}^{(K)})}^{2}=\Bigl(1-\sqrt{1+\tfrac{t^{2}}{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}}\Bigr)^{2}\leq t^{4}I_{k,P^{(K)}}^{2},

which completes the proof.
∎

We now show that the sequence of least favorable submodels based on the Le Cam–Hájek QMD path is Fisher-normalizable.

Lemma 18

(Fisher-normalizability of Le Cam–Hájek least favorable submodels)
.

Let
P_{t}^{(K)}
denote the product submodel through
P^{(K)}
, defined by factorizing the joint density as

\frac{dP_{t}^{(K)}}{d\nu^{(K)}}(O^{(K)})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\prod_{i=1}^{n_{k}}p_{k,t}^{(K)}(O_{ki}),

where, for each
1\leq k\leq K
, the path
t\mapsto p_{k,t}^{(K)}
is the Le Cam–Hájek QMD submodel through the marginal density
p_{k}^{(K)}
of
P^{(K)}
, with score function
o_{ki}\mapsto\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(o_{ki})
. Suppose

\max_{1\leq k\leq K}\operatorname{Var}_{P^{(K)}}\left(\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki})\right)=o(N).

Then, for any
\delta>0
, the sequence of submodels
\{P_{t}^{(K)}:|t|\leq\delta\}_{K=1}^{\infty}
is a Fisher-normalizable sequence of least favorable submodels.

Proof.

By Lemma
17
, the Le Cam–Hájek submodel is QMD with remainder

\left\|\sqrt{p_{k,t}^{(K)}}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}-\tfrac{t}{2}\,s_{k,P^{(K)}}\,\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{k}^{(K)})}^{2}=O(I_{k,K}^{2}t^{4}),

where
I_{k,K}:=\operatorname{Var}_{P^{(K)}}(\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki}))
. Relabeling
t:=tI_{K}^{-1/2}
, we find

\left\|\sqrt{p_{k,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}-\tfrac{t}{2}\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}\,\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{k}^{(K)})}^{2}=\frac{I_{k,K}^{2}}{I_{K}^{2}}O(t^{4}).

Averaging over
k
and noting that
I_{K}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}I_{k,K}
, we obtain, for each fixed
|t|\leq\delta
, as
K\rightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}\left\|\sqrt{p_{k,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}-\tfrac{t}{2}\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}\,\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{k}^{(K)})}^{2}

\displaystyle=O(t^{4})\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}\frac{I_{k,K}^{2}}{I_{K}^{2}}

\displaystyle=O(t^{4})I_{K}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}I_{k,K}^{2}}{N\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}I_{k,K}}

\displaystyle\leq O(t^{4})I_{K}^{-1}\cdot\frac{\max_{1\leq k\leq K}I_{k,K}}{N}

\displaystyle=o(I_{K}^{-1}),

since by assumption
\max_{1\leq k\leq K}I_{k,K}/N=o(1)
. Hence, the submodel is a Fisher-normalizable sequence of least favorable submodels.
∎

I.3
Local asymptotic normality along least favorable submodels

The following theorem establishes local asymptotic normality (LAN) along a sequence of Fisher-normalizable least favorable submodels as
K\to\infty
and
N:=N(K)\to\infty
. The proof closely follows Theorem 7.2 in
Van der Vaart (2000)
, which establishes LAN in the i.i.d. case. To generalize to the many-weak-instruments regime, it relies on the Fisher-normalizable property in Definition
1
, which ensures that the quadratic mean differentiability (QMD) remainder vanishes after normalization by the total Fisher information.

Theorem 17

(LAN for Fisher-normalizable least favorable submodels)
.

Let
\{(P_{t}^{(K)}:|t|\leq\delta)\}_{K=1}^{\infty}
be a Fisher-normalizable sequence of least favorable submodels satisfying Definition
1
through
P^{(K)}
. Suppose the following hold as
K\to\infty
:

(i)

I_{K}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\operatorname{Var}_{P^{(K)}}(\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki}))\to\infty
;

(ii)

N:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}
satisfies
N/I_{K}=O(1)
;

(iii)

(Lindeberg condition)

\frac{1}{I_{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}E_{P^{(K)}}\left[\varphi^{*2}_{P^{(K)}}(O_{ki})\cdot\mathbf{1}\left\{\varphi^{*2}_{P^{(K)}}(O_{ki})>\tfrac{I_{K}}{t^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\right\}\right]\to 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all }\varepsilon>0.

Then the submodel is locally asymptotically normal, in the sense that

\log\frac{dP_{tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}{dP^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}=t\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki})-\frac{t^{2}}{2}+o_{p}(1),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }K\to\infty.

Moreover, by the Lindeberg central limit theorem,

\log\frac{p_{t}^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}{p^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}\left(-\tfrac{t^{2}}{2},\,t^{2}\right).

Proof.

Following the proof of Theorem 7.2 in
Van der Vaart (2000)
, define the random variable

W_{ki}:=2\left[\sqrt{\frac{p_{k,\,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}(O_{ki})}{p_{k}^{(K)}(O_{ki})}}-1\right].

Then the log-likelihood ratio admits the expansion

\log\frac{p_{t}^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}{p^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}=2\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\log\left(1+\tfrac{1}{2}W_{ki}\right).

(16)

We aim to show that, as
K\to\infty
,

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}=t\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki})-\frac{t^{2}}{4}+o_{p}(1).

(17)

To prove this, it suffices to show that both the mean and the variance of the difference

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}-t\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki})-\frac{t^{2}}{4}

converge to zero. We begin with the variance:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Var}_{P^{(K)}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}-t\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\,\operatorname{Var}_{P_{k}^{(K)}}\left(W_{ki}-t\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\int\left\{\sqrt{\frac{p_{k,\,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}(o)}{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}}-1-\tfrac{t}{2}I_{K}^{-1/2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(o)\right\}^{2}dP_{k}^{(K)}(o).

Denote the upper bound by
RHS
. Switching to integration with respect to
\nu_{k}^{(K)}
, we obtain:

RHS

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\int\left\{\sqrt{p_{k,\,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}(o)}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}-\tfrac{t}{2}I_{K}^{-1/2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(o)\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}\right\}^{2}d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{I_{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}I_{K}\int\left\{\sqrt{p_{k,\,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}(o)}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}-\tfrac{t}{2}I_{K}^{-1/2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(o)\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}\right\}^{2}d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o).

By assumption,
N/I_{K}=O(1)
as
K\to\infty
. Moreover, by Fisher-normalizability of the least favorable submodel sequence (Property 2 of Definition
1
), the weighted average term is
o(1)
. Hence,

\text{RHS}=O(1)\cdot o(1)=o(1),

so the variance vanishes.

Turning to the expectation, we compute:

\displaystyle E_{P^{(K)}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}\right]

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\left(\int\sqrt{p_{k,\,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}(o)}\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}\,d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)-1\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\int\left(\sqrt{p_{k,\,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}(o)}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}\right)^{2}d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o)

\displaystyle=-\frac{N}{I_{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}I_{K}\int\left(\sqrt{p_{k,\,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}(o)}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}(o)}\right)^{2}d\nu_{k}^{(K)}(o).

Applying the second-order QMD expansion (again from Property 2 of Definition
1
), we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}I_{K}\int\left\{\sqrt{p_{k,\,tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}}-\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}\right\}^{2}d\nu_{k}^{(K)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}\int\left(\tfrac{t}{2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}\sqrt{p_{k}^{(K)}}\right)^{2}d\nu_{k}^{(K)}+o(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{2}}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}\operatorname{Var}_{P_{k}^{(K)}}(\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki}))+o(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{I_{K}}{N}+o(1).

Substituting this into the previous display and using that
\frac{N}{I_{K}}=O(1)
gives:

E_{P^{(K)}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}\right]=-\frac{t^{2}}{4}+o(1).

We conclude that both the mean and variance of the difference converge to zero, completing the proof of (
17
).

We now return to equation (
16
). Following equation (7.5) in
Van der Vaart (2000)
, a Taylor expansion yields
\log(1 + x)=x-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}+x^{2}R(2x),

where
R(x)\to 0
as
x\to 0
. Hence,

\displaystyle\log\frac{p_{t}^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}{p^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\log\left(1+\tfrac{1}{2}W_{ki}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}^{2}R(W_{ki}).

(18)

By Property 2 of Definition
1
, we can write

I_{K}W_{ki}^{2}=t^{2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})+A_{ki},

for a random variable
A_{ki}
such that
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{n_{k}}{N}E_{P^{(K)}}[\,|A_{ki}|\,]\longrightarrow 0\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }K\to\infty.

By linearity of expectation, it follows that the sum
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}A_{ki}
converges to zero in expectation, and hence in probability. Thus, as
K\rightarrow\infty
, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}^{2}

\displaystyle=t^{2}I_{K}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})+\frac{N}{I_{K}}\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}A_{ki}

\displaystyle=t^{2}I_{K}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})+O_{p}(1)o_{p}(1),

where in the final equality we use the assumption that
\frac{N}{I_{K}}=O_{p}(1)
. Thus, by the law of large numbers for sums of independent random variables, it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}^{2}=t^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\frac{\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})}{I_{K}^{-1}}+o_{p}(1)\longrightarrow_{p}t^{2}.

Arguing again as in
Van der Vaart (2000)
, and applying the triangle inequality and Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle P\left(\max_{ki}|W_{ki}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}P^{(K)}\left(t^{2}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})>I_{K}\varepsilon^{2}\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}P^{(K)}\left(|A_{ki}|>I_{K}\varepsilon^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-2}\cdot\frac{t^{2}}{I_{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}E_{P^{(K)}}\left[\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})\cdot\mathbf{1}\left\{\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})>\tfrac{I_{K}}{t^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\varepsilon^{-2}\cdot\frac{1}{I_{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}E_{P^{(K)}}\left[|A_{ki}|\right]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2}\cdot\frac{t^{2}}{I_{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}E_{P^{(K)}}\left[\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})\cdot\mathbf{1}\left\{\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})>\tfrac{I_{K}}{t^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\right\}\right]+\varepsilon^{-2}\cdot\frac{1}{I_{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}E_{P^{(K)}}\left[|A_{ki}|\right]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2}\cdot\frac{t^{2}}{I_{K}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}E_{P^{(K)}}\left[\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})\cdot\mathbf{1}\left\{\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*2}(O_{ki})>\tfrac{I_{K}}{t^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\right\}\right]+\varepsilon^{-2}\cdot o_{p}(1),

where we use that
\frac{N}{I_{K}}=O_{p}(1)
, apply the Lindeberg condition, and use that
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}E_{P^{(K)}}\left[|A_{ki}|\right]=o_{p}(1)
.
In the proof of Theorem 7.2 of
Van der Vaart (2000)
, it is shown that
\max_{ki}|W_{ki}|=o_{p}(1)
implies
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}^{2}R(W_{ki})=o_{p}(1)
. Combining this with the fact that
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}^{2}\rightarrow_{p}t^{2}
, and using the asymptotic expansion for
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}W_{ki}
in (
17
), we plug into (
18
) to obtain

\displaystyle\log\frac{p_{t}^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}{p^{(K)}(O^{(K)})}

\displaystyle=t\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki})-\frac{t^{2}}{4}-\frac{t^{2}}{4}+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle=t\,I_{K}^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi_{P^{(K)}}^{*}(O_{ki})-\frac{t^{2}}{2}+o_{p}(1),

as desired.

∎

I.4
Proofs for Section
7

Proof of Theorem
10
.

The conditions of Theorem
10
imply those of Theorem
17
. The result then follows directly from Theorem
17
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
11
.

The theorem is a direct consequence of the general convolution theorem for locally asymptotically normal experiments, as established in
Van der Vaart (2000, Theorems 25.22–25.23)
. We outline the key steps of the proof here.
Define

T_{K}=\sqrt{N/\sigma_{K}^{*2}}\,\bigl(\widehat{\psi}_{K}-\Psi^{(K)}(P^{(K)})\bigr),\hskip 18.49988ptZ_{K}=I_{K}^{-1/2}\sum_{k,i}\varphi^{*}_{P^{(K)}}(O_{ki}),

and let
\Lambda_{K}(u)=\log\tfrac{dP^{(K)}_{uI_{K}^{-1/2}}}{dP^{(K)}}
. By Theorem
10
, we have

\Lambda_{K}(u)=uZ_{K}-\tfrac{u^{2}}{2}+o_{P^{(K)}}(1),\hskip 9.24994ptZ_{K}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1),

which implies that the sequence
\{P^{(K)}_{uI_{K}^{-1/2}}\}
is contiguous with respect to
P^{(K)}
.

Regularity (Definition
2
) ensures that for each fixed
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

T_{K}-u=\sqrt{N/\sigma_{K}^{*2}}\,\bigl(\widehat{\psi}_{K}-\Psi^{(K)}(P^{(K)}_{uI_{K}^{-1/2}})\bigr)\xrightarrow{d}L\hskip 9.24994pt\text{under }P^{(K)}_{uI_{K}^{-1/2}},

for some limiting distribution
L
that does not depend on
u
. Hence, under
P^{(K)}
,

(T_{K}-u,\;\Lambda_{K}(u))\xrightarrow{d}(L,\;uZ-\tfrac{u^{2}}{2}),

and by Le Cam’s third lemma (see van der Vaart, 1998, Thm. 25.22–25.23), it follows that

(T_{K},\;Z_{K})\xrightarrow{d}(L+Z,\;Z),\hskip 9.24994ptZ\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),\;Z\perp L.

Projecting onto the first coordinate yields
T_{K}\xrightarrow{d}L+Z
. Since
Z
is independent of
L
, we have
\operatorname{Var}(T_{K})=\operatorname{Var}(L)+1\geq 1
, with equality if and only if
L\equiv 0
, i.e.,
T_{K}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
12
.

Let
O^{(K)}=\{O_{ki}:1\leq k\leq K,\;1\leq i\leq n_{k}\}
,
N=\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}
, and

\bar{\varphi}_{K}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\varphi^{*}_{P^{(K)}}(O_{ki}).

By the asymptotically linear expansion, multiplying both sides by
\sqrt{N/\sigma_{K}^{*2}}
gives

\sqrt{\tfrac{N}{\sigma_{K}^{*2}}}\bigl(\widehat{\psi}_{K}-\Psi^{(K)}(P^{(K)})\bigr)=I_{K}^{-1/2}\,\bar{\varphi}_{K}+o_{P^{(K)}}(1).

Next, by Theorem
10
, for each fixed
u
, the log-likelihood ratio admits the LAN expansion

\log\frac{dP_{u}^{(K)}}{dP^{(K)}}(O^{(K)})=u\,\frac{\bar{\varphi}_{K}}{\sqrt{I_{K}}}-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+o_{P^{(K)}}(1).

Since
\bar{\varphi}_{K}/\sqrt{I_{K}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
under
P^{(K)}
, this expansion—together with the QMD property and the central limit theorem—implies that the shifted laws
P_{uI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}
are contiguous with respect to
P^{(K)}

(
Van der Vaart, 2000
)
. Therefore, by Le Cam’s third lemma,

\frac{\bar{\varphi}_{K}}{\sqrt{I_{K}}}\;\xrightarrow{d}\;\mathcal{N}(u,1)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{under }P_{uI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}.

By
F3
,

\tfrac{d}{du}\Psi^{(K)}(P_{u}^{(K)})\big|_{u=0}=I_{K}/N
,
so a Taylor expansion gives for fixed
t

\Psi^{(K)}\bigl(P_{tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}\bigr)=\Psi^{(K)}(P^{(K)})+t\,\frac{\sqrt{I_{K}}}{N}+o\!\bigl(t\sigma_{K}^{*}N^{-1/2}\bigr).

Hence under
P_{tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)}
, we have

\sqrt{\tfrac{N}{\sigma_{K}^{*2}}}\bigl(\widehat{\psi}_{K}-\Psi^{(K)}(P_{tI_{K}^{-1/2}}^{(K)})\bigr)=I_{K}^{-1/2}\,\bar{\varphi}_{K}-t+o(1)\;\xrightarrow{d}\;\mathcal{\mathcal{}}{N}(0,1),

where the final convergence follows from (
I.4
).
∎
